Entheon & Kaleo Master Reference

This document defines the canonical creature systems and world reference for Kaleo. It is intended as the authoritative source for Entheon species, combat, progression, items, Trainer systems, geography, society and related setting rules.

Complete canonical reference. For easier reading, use the section pages from the navigation instead of scrolling this entire document.

Part I — Core Systems

Creature Combat System

1. Purpose

The Creature Combat System defines how creatures battle, develop, learn techniques, use innate abilities, and interact with Affinities. The system is intentionally structured similarly to established creature-battling RPG systems. It uses familiar concepts such as creature statistics, elemental affinities, Physical/Special/Status techniques, abilities, learnsets, type effectiveness, status conditions, and evolutionary progression.

2. Creature Data

Every creature species has the following core combat information: - Species Name - Affinity - Rarity - Evolution Stage - Evolution Line - Base Statistics - Innate Abilities - Signature Moves - Level-Up Moves - Technique/TM Compatibility - Move Compatibility Restrictions - Evolution-Related Moves or Abilities A creature’s combat capabilities should be derived from its established species design, Affinity, anatomy, evolution and documented abilities. A creature cannot use abilities, moves or powers that contradict its established species data.

3. Creature Statistics

Every creature possesses six primary combat statistics:

HP — Health Points

Determines how much damage a creature can sustain before it is unable to continue battling.

Attack

Determines the effectiveness of primarily Physical attacks.

Defense

Determines the creature’s ability to withstand primarily Physical attacks.

Special Attack

Determines the effectiveness of primarily Special attacks.

Special Defense

Determines the creature’s ability to withstand primarily Special attacks.

Speed

Determines how quickly the creature can act in battle and influences turn order.

4. Combat Categories

Every damaging technique belongs to one of two primary damage categories.

Physical

Physical techniques represent attacks relying primarily on the creature’s physical body, strength, claws, teeth, limbs, wings, weapons, or other direct physical mechanisms. Physical damage is primarily determined by:

Attacker’s Attack → Defender’s Defense

Examples include: - Bites - Claw attacks - Tackles - Wing strikes - Tail strikes - Physical Flame attacks - Physical Volt attacks

Special

Special techniques represent attacks relying primarily on Affinity energy, ranged projection, supernatural abilities, elemental manipulation, psychic effects, or other non-physical mechanisms. Special damage is primarily determined by:

Attacker’s Special Attack → Defender’s Special Defense

Examples include: - Flame projectiles - Lightning attacks - Water techniques - Psychic/Mystic techniques - Radiant beams - Umbral energy attacks A technique’s Affinity does not determine whether it is Physical or Special. Both Physical and Special techniques can exist within the same Affinity.

5. Status Techniques

Status techniques do not primarily deal direct damage. Instead, they alter the battle through effects such as: - Increasing or decreasing statistics - Inflicting status conditions - Healing - Protecting the user - Altering the battlefield - Altering weather - Creating Affinity-based fields - Applying temporary advantages or disadvantages - Manipulating turn order - Restricting or disrupting opposing creatures Status techniques may belong to an Affinity or may be Neutral/General techniques.

6. Technique Data

Every technique has a defined data entry containing: - Name - Affinity - Category - Power - Accuracy - PP - Priority - Effect - Target - Learn Method - Species Compatibility, where applicable

Power

Determines the base strength of a damaging technique. Status techniques normally have no Power value.

Accuracy

Determines the probability that a technique successfully affects its intended target. Certain techniques may intentionally have imperfect accuracy in exchange for greater power or stronger effects.

PP

Determines how many times a technique can normally be used before requiring recovery or restoration.

Priority

Determines whether a technique can act before or after normal turn order. Most techniques have normal priority. Certain techniques may have increased or decreased priority as part of their design.

7. Affinities

Every creature possesses one or two Affinities. The established Affinities are: 1. Flame 2. Tide 3. Verdant 4. Gale 5. Volt 6. Frost 7. Stone 8. Metal 9. Mystic 10. Radiant 11. Umbral 12. Wild 13. Dragon A creature’s Affinity represents an inherent aspect of its biology, energy, abilities and combat identity. A creature’s Affinity should influence its available techniques and abilities, but should not completely determine its visual appearance, personality or combat style.

8. Single-Affinity Creatures

A single-Affinity creature possesses one Affinity. Example:

Quiblet

Affinity: Flame Quiblet receives the benefits associated with Flame and has access to appropriate Flame techniques. Its anatomy and established species design determine which Physical, Status and General techniques it can additionally learn.

9. Dual-Affinity Creatures

A dual-Affinity creature possesses two Affinities. Example:

Quivane

Affinity: Flame / Volt A dual-Affinity creature may use techniques associated with either of its Affinities and receives the same-affinity benefit when using an appropriate technique. Dual-Affinity creatures do not automatically gain every technique belonging to either Affinity. Technique compatibility remains dependent upon species, anatomy and established learnsets.

10. Same-Affinity Technique Bonus

A creature receives a Same-Affinity Bonus when using a technique that matches one of its own Affinities. For a single-Affinity creature: > Creature: Flame >  Technique: Flame >  Result: Same-Affinity Bonus For a dual-Affinity creature: > Creature: Flame / Volt >  Technique: Flame >  Result: Same-Affinity Bonus > Creature: Flame / Volt >  Technique: Volt >  Result: Same-Affinity Bonus > Creature: Flame / Volt >  Technique: Tide >  Result: No Same-Affinity Bonus The exact numerical value of the Same-Affinity Bonus will be established separately.

11. Affinity Effectiveness

Affinities interact with one another during combat. Every Affinity matchup will eventually be assigned an effectiveness value. The planned categories are: - Super Effective - Effective - Neutral - Resisted - Highly Resisted - Immune, where appropriate The complete Affinity interaction chart will be established separately. Battles use the documented Affinity interaction rules rather than inventing effectiveness relationships during individual battles.

12. Abilities

Abilities represent innate characteristics that affect how a creature behaves or performs in battle. An ability may be: - Passive - Triggered by a specific condition - Triggered when entering battle - Triggered when the creature’s HP reaches a threshold - Triggered by a particular Affinity interaction - Triggered by a specific battlefield condition - Activated through a specific combat event Each species should normally possess one or two standard abilities. Some species may additionally possess a Hidden Ability. Abilities should reinforce the established species identity rather than function as arbitrary additional powers.

13. Signature Moves

Every species may possess one to three Signature Moves. Signature Moves are techniques strongly associated with a particular species and are not intended to be interchangeable with generic Affinity techniques. A Signature Move should reflect at least one of the following: - The creature’s anatomy - Its Affinity - Its personality - Its ecological behaviour - Its evolutionary identity - Its distinctive combat style Example:

Sindra

Signature Move: Shadowflame Rush   Affinity: Flame / Umbral   Category: Physical The exact mechanics of individual Signature Moves will be established within the species’ own data entry.

14. General Affinity Move Pools

Each Affinity possesses a shared pool of General Techniques. These techniques represent commonly occurring techniques associated with that Affinity. For example, the Flame Move Pool may contain: - Basic Flame techniques - Physical Flame techniques - Special Flame techniques - Flame Status techniques - Defensive Flame techniques - Utility Flame techniques Species may learn appropriate techniques from their Affinity pools according to their anatomy, evolution, rarity and individual learnset. Possessing an Affinity does not automatically grant access to every technique within that Affinity’s move pool.

15. General/Neutral Move Pool

A separate General Move Pool exists for techniques that are not intrinsically tied to a specific Affinity. Examples may include: - Basic physical attacks - Defensive techniques - Movement techniques - Recovery techniques - Focus techniques - Utility techniques Compatibility depends on species anatomy and established learnsets. For example, an avian creature may learn appropriate Wing-based techniques, while a creature without wings cannot.

16. Anatomical Compatibility

A creature’s physical anatomy determines which techniques it can reasonably learn. Examples: - Creatures with wings may learn appropriate wing or aerial techniques. - Creatures with claws may learn claw-based techniques. - Creatures with fangs may learn bite-based techniques. - Serpentine creatures may learn constriction or coil-based techniques. - Aquatic creatures may possess techniques requiring water-based movement. - Creatures without limbs cannot arbitrarily learn techniques requiring conventional limbs. An Entheon cannot use an anatomically impossible technique unless its species documentation explicitly establishes the necessary ability.

17. Evolution and Techniques

Evolution represents progression rather than replacement. When a creature evolves, it normally retains previously learned techniques unless an established species mechanic specifically states otherwise. An evolved creature may: - Retain previous techniques - Learn stronger versions of existing techniques - Gain new techniques - Gain access to additional Affinity techniques - Gain new Signature Moves - Develop new abilities - Replace or modify certain abilities where explicitly documented Evolution should produce a recognizable progression from the previous stage rather than creating an unrelated moveset.

18. Evolution Affinity Rules

For standard three-stage evolutionary lines, the established Affinity progression must be respected. A creature may acquire a second Affinity during evolution. Once a second Affinity has been acquired, it remains consistent through subsequent stages unless the species is explicitly documented as having a special branching or replacement evolution mechanic. Example: > Stage 1: Flame >  Stage 2: Flame / Volt >  Stage 3: Flame / Volt The Stage 3 creature does not arbitrarily change to a different Affinity. Special evolutionary lines may intentionally use different rules when explicitly documented.

19. Branching Evolution

Certain species may possess multiple possible evolutionary forms. A branching evolutionary species may replace or alter its original Affinity depending on the evolutionary path. This is an intentional exception to the standard evolutionary Affinity rule. The individual branch must explicitly document: - Evolution condition - Resulting species - Resulting Affinity - New abilities - New Signature Moves - Changes to the learnset The Eevee-style concept established for one two-stage line is an example of a species where the base Affinity may be replaced by the chosen evolution.

20. Move Acquisition

Techniques may be acquired through several methods.

Starting Moves

Techniques available when a creature first becomes battle-ready.

Level-Up Moves

Techniques learned naturally as the creature grows stronger.

Evolution Moves

Techniques gained upon reaching a new evolutionary stage.

Signature Moves

Species-specific techniques associated with the creature’s unique identity.

Technique/TM Compatibility

Techniques that can be deliberately taught to compatible creatures.

Special Techniques

Techniques obtained through unusual circumstances, training, story events or other documented methods.

21. Learnset Philosophy

A creature’s complete learnset should reflect its identity. The learnset should consider: - Affinity - Anatomy - Evolution - Rarity - Personality - Ecological role - Established combat identity A creature should not receive a technique solely because that technique exists within one of its Affinities. Likewise, two creatures sharing an Affinity should not necessarily have identical movesets. Their Signature Moves and learnsets should provide meaningful differentiation.

22. Status Conditions

The system supports major and minor combat conditions.

Major Conditions

  • Burn
  • Poison
  • Paralysis
  • Sleep
  • Freeze

Secondary Conditions and Effects

  • Confusion
  • Flinching
  • Accuracy reduction
  • Evasion reduction
  • Attack reduction
  • Defense reduction
  • Special Attack reduction
  • Special Defense reduction
  • Speed reduction
  • Other temporary combat effects The exact rules and durations for each condition will be defined separately.

23. Battlefield Effects

Certain techniques and abilities may alter the battlefield. Possible battlefield mechanics include:

Weather

Examples: - Rain - Sun - Snow - Storm

Affinity Fields

Examples: - Radiant Field - Umbral Field - Verdant Field - Mystic Field Battlefield effects may: - Strengthen specific Affinity techniques - Weaken specific Affinity techniques - Alter creature abilities - Alter move effects - Apply environmental conditions - Change strategic priorities The complete battlefield system will be defined separately.

Move Database

Purpose

This document establishes the reusable move pool for the 13 creature Affinities and the General/Neutral pool. These moves form the shared mechanical vocabulary from which individual species learnsets will be constructed. This version deliberately treats moves as a complete combat toolkit, rather than as an attack catalogue. Offensive attacks, Status moves, utility, defensive techniques, recovery, cleansing, setup and field interaction are all represented. Species-specific Signature Moves are intentionally excluded and will be established separately.

Move Data Fields

Every move entry contains: - Move Name - Affinity - Category - Power - Accuracy - PP - Priority - Target - Effect

Category

  • Physical — uses the user’s physical offensive capability.
  • Special — uses the user’s special offensive capability.
  • Status — does not directly deal damage and instead produces a condition, buff, debuff, recovery effect, field effect or other utility.

Power

The base offensive strength of a damaging move. Status moves have no Power value.

Accuracy

The base probability that a move successfully affects its intended target.

PP

The normal number of times the move may be used before requiring restoration.

Priority

Determines whether a move acts before or after normal turn order.

Target

Defines the intended target pattern, such as: - Single opponent - All opponents - User - Ally - User or ally - Allied side - Field - All combatants

Move Pool Design Philosophy

The Affinity pools are toolkits, not lists of attacks. Individual Affinities should have recognizable mechanical identities. A typical Affinity pool should contain a mixture of: - Basic offensive moves - Stronger offensive moves - Physical and/or Special options where appropriate - Status moves - Buffs and debuffs - Defensive or utility techniques - Recovery or sustain where thematically appropriate - Field/weather interaction where appropriate - One or more distinctive mechanical effects Not every Affinity needs access to direct HP recovery. In particular: - Verdant specializes in natural regeneration and vitality. - Radiant specializes in restoration, purification and protective light. - Mystic specializes in arcane/psychic restoration and mental resilience. - Other Affinities can specialize in defense, speed, disruption, offense, battlefield control or other identities instead of receiving generic healing. The exact number of moves in each pool may be expanded later.

Flame emphasizes offensive pressure, burning effects, momentum and high-output attacks.

Recovery Philosophy

Recovery should not be treated as a mandatory feature of every Affinity. Instead, recovery should help establish mechanical identity.

Verdant — Regeneration

Verdant recovery represents natural life force, growth and biological renewal. Verdant recovery can: - Restore HP directly - Restore HP over multiple turns - Become stronger under Verdant field effects - Interact with draining or plant-based techniques

Radiant — Restoration and Purification

Radiant recovery represents cleansing, healing light and restoration. Radiant recovery can: - Restore HP - Remove negative status conditions - Protect against further negative effects - Support allies - Interact with Radiant field effects

Mystic — Arcane Restoration

Mystic recovery represents psychic focus, magical energy and mental resilience. Mystic recovery can: - Restore HP - Strengthen Special Defense - Protect against mental/status disruption - Restore allies through controlled energy - Interact with Mystic field effects

General — Basic Recovery

General recovery provides universally understandable survival tools without giving a creature a specialized Affinity identity. Recover, Rest, Endure and similar moves belong here.

Other Affinities

Other Affinities are not required to possess direct healing. They can instead sustain themselves through: - Damage mitigation - Defensive buffs - Drain effects - Speed and evasion - Field control - Status disruption - Recoil tradeoffs - Offensive pressure This prevents every Affinity from becoming mechanically interchangeable.

Affinity Identity Summary

Affinity Primary Mechanical Identity
Flame Offensive pressure, Burn, momentum
Tide Flow, cleansing, control, defensive adaptability
Verdant Regeneration, growth, draining, vitality
Gale Speed, mobility, evasion, aerial control
Volt Speed, paralysis, burst damage, field control
Frost Slowing, freezing, defense, control
Stone Durability, barriers, heavy impact
Metal Armor, precision, defense, controlled power
Mystic Special power, mental effects, arcane restoration
Radiant Purification, healing, protection, illumination
Umbral Debuffs, concealment, disruption, draining
Wild Physical ability, instinct, adaptability, survival
Dragon Ancient power, overwhelming force, intimidation

Move Pool Design Rules

Affinity Access

A creature may learn techniques from: - Its own Affinity or Affinities - The General Move Pool - Other Affinity pools where species compatibility is explicitly established Possessing an Affinity does not automatically grant every move within that Affinity.

Anatomy

Species anatomy determines compatibility with physical techniques. A wingless creature should not automatically learn wing-based techniques. A creature without suitable limbs, fangs, horns, claws, etc. should not automatically learn techniques that require those anatomical features.

Evolution

Evolution may unlock additional techniques while retaining previously learned compatible moves. A change in Affinity may expand or alter the available move pool.

Signature Moves

Species-specific Signature Moves are separate from this global database. Signature Moves should reinforce the creature’s identity rather than simply duplicate an existing generic move.

Balance

Move Power, Accuracy and PP should be considered together. High-power techniques generally have one or more balancing factors such as: - Lower Accuracy - Low PP - Recoil - Stat reduction - Preparation requirements - Conditional availability - Self-debuffs - Strong defensive counterplay

Species Learnsets

The global move pool is a source catalogue. An individual species should receive a small curated learnset, not the entire pool. Its learnset should reflect: - Affinity - Anatomy - Species concept - Evolution stage - Combat identity - Rarity - Personality or lore where appropriate

Reserved for Future Expansion

  1. Complete Affinity effectiveness chart
  2. Exact Same-Affinity Bonus
  3. Status condition mechanics
  4. Weather mechanics
  5. Affinity field mechanics
  6. Critical-hit mechanics
  7. Exact damage formula
  8. Species-specific Signature Moves
  9. Individual species learnsets
  10. Technique/TM compatibility lists
  11. Level-up progression
  12. Evolution-specific move acquisition
  13. Ally-support and multi-target rules
  14. Exact recovery percentages and formulas

Data Integrity Rule

The move names, properties, effects and narrative descriptions in this document constitute the canonical reusable move pool once this version is approved. Species-specific exceptions or modifications must be explicitly documented in the relevant species entry.

Special Move & Ability System

1. Purpose

Special Moves and Special Abilities are distinctive traits that give individual Entheon additional mechanical identity beyond their standard move pool, Affinity and statistics. A Special does not automatically mean an Entheon is stronger. A Special may provide: - Offensive effects - Defensive effects - Recovery - Battlefield control - Mobility - Utility - Status interaction - Affinity interaction - Support - Environmental interaction - A unique passive effect Some Specials may be highly powerful in specific situations, while others may be subtle but useful.

2. Individual Variation

Special Moves and Special Abilities are not automatically possessed by every Entheon of a species. A species may have a designated Special Move or Special Ability that an individual Entheon can possess, without every member of that species having it. This creates meaningful individual variation between Entheon of the same species. For example: > Tarnelle A — Stone/Metal — No Special >  Tarnelle B — Stone/Metal — Stoneheart Both are valid Tarnelle. Their other characteristics may also differ. A trainer may therefore encounter a wild Entheon that possesses a Special while their own Entheon of the same species does not. This can create meaningful reasons to consider catching and training another individual of a species.

3. Special Acquisition Through Evolution

For ordinary evolutionary lines, gaining a Special upon evolution is possible but not guaranteed. An Entheon that evolves into a form eligible for a species-specific Special may: - Gain the Special upon evolution; or - Evolve without gaining the Special. The Special is therefore an individual trait, not an automatic evolutionary reward.

Existing Special

If an Entheon already possesses a Special before evolving, that Special is retained. When the Special has an evolved-form version, the existing Special may develop into that evolved version. Example: > Tarnelle with Stoneheart >  ↓ Evolution >  Tarnovar with Living Bastion However: > Tarnelle without a Special >  ↓ Evolution >  Tarnovar without a Special is also possible.

4. Starter Exception

The three starter Entheon are a deliberate exception to the normal acquisition rules. Each starter line has a designated Special Ability from Stage 1. The starter’s Special: - Is present from the beginning. - Is retained through evolution. - May develop or become more sophisticated with each evolution. The three starter lines therefore begin with an immediately recognizable mechanical identity. This exception applies specifically to the three starter species and does not establish a general rule that all Stage 1 Entheon possess Specials.

5. Common Entheon

Common Entheon generally do not begin with a Special Move or Special Ability. For Common Entheon belonging to evolutionary lines: - Stage 1 normally has no Special. - An evolved form may gain its species’ designated Special. - Gaining that Special upon evolution is not guaranteed. - An individual that already possesses a Special retains it when evolving. The purpose is to make Special acquisition a potential discovery rather than an automatic part of evolution.

6. Uncommon Entheon

Uncommon Entheon may possess a Special Move or Special Ability. For evolutionary lines, the Special may first appear upon evolution. Possession remains individual rather than guaranteed unless the species is specifically defined otherwise.

7. Rare Entheon

Rare Entheon are generally more likely to possess distinctive Specials. Rare evolutionary forms may have a designated Special that an individual can acquire upon reaching that evolutionary stage. However, rarity alone does not guarantee possession unless explicitly stated for that species.

8. Extremely Rare Entheon

Extremely Rare Entheon normally have a distinctive Special Move or Special Ability associated with their species. Their Special does not automatically need to be overwhelmingly powerful. It may instead provide an unusual mechanic, strong utility, battlefield control, support, defense or another defining characteristic.

9. Legendary Entheon

Legendary Entheon always possess their defining Special Move or Special Ability. A Legendary Special should reinforce the identity of the Legendary Entheon rather than simply functioning as an extremely powerful attack. A Legendary Special may be: - Offensive - Defensive - Supportive - Environmental - Utility-based - Passive - Battlefield-oriented - Or a combination of these Legendary status does not require the Special itself to be an offensive powerhouse.

10. Discovering Specials

Specials should be discoverable through observation and interaction. A trainer should be able to recognize when an Entheon demonstrates an unusual Special during battle. This allows wild encounters to create meaningful moments where a trainer realizes that an individual Entheon possesses a particular Special. For example: > A wild Tarnelle withstands an attack and its rocky body visibly hardens. The trainer can recognize that this Tarnelle possesses Stoneheart. This prevents Special acquisition from becoming purely invisible randomness while still allowing individual Entheon to differ.

Special Ability / Move Roster

The following roster defines the designated Special concept for eligible Entheon. It does not mean every individual of that species automatically possesses the Special.

Auralith’s immense body generates a stable field of Radiant/Mystic energy. While stationary, its floating monolithic fragments gradually arrange themselves into increasingly complex formations that can provide different defensive or supportive effects.

098 — Vaelora

Radiant/Volt — Legendary

Vaelora draws energy from atmospheric electricity while airborne. As electrical energy accumulates, its Radiant aura intensifies, allowing it to alternate between electrical and Radiant manifestations.

099 — Nethryss

Umbral/Dragon — Legendary

Passive Special Ability

Nethryss’s overwhelming presence imposes a profound instinctive fear upon opposing Entheon.

Core effect:

 Lowers opposing Attack even against Entheon that are normally immune to standard intimidation-based Attack reduction.

Special Rule:

Absolute Presence bypasses immunity to standard Attack-lowering intimidation effects. This exception applies only to Absolute Presence.

100 — Orrytheon

Mystic/Metal — Legendary

Special Ability: Astral Alignment

Orrytheon’s metallic rings and orbiting structures continuously adjust around its body. When it uses Mystic or Metal techniques, the rings can temporarily realign to alter the technique’s defensive or offensive characteristics.

Special System Design Principles

The Special system should follow these principles: 1. A Special is not guaranteed merely because a species has a designated Special. 2. Specials are individual traits. 3. Evolution can reveal a Special, but evolution does not guarantee one. 4. If an individual already has a Special, evolution does not remove it. 5. A Special may evolve or become more sophisticated when its owner evolves. 6. The three starter Entheon are the explicit Stage 1 exception. 7. Common Stage 1 Entheon normally have no Special. 8. Rare, Extremely Rare and Legendary Entheon are increasingly likely to possess distinctive Specials, but only Legendary Entheon are guaranteed to possess their defining Special. 9. A Special does not inherently indicate greater combat power. 10. Specials should create meaningful tactical or individual differences rather than simply increase damage. 11. Special activation mechanics, frequency, duration, stacking and numerical effects must be defined separately during the balancing phase. 12. A trainer should be able to recognize a Special when an individual Entheon demonstrates it in battle. 13. Different individuals of the same species may therefore have meaningfully different mechanical identities. 14. A trainer may have a legitimate reason to catch another individual of a species if it demonstrates a Special their current Entheon lacks. 15. The system should avoid rewarding trainers for deliberately allowing their Entheon to take unnecessary damage solely to activate a Special.

Nature & Personality System

1. Purpose

The Nature and Personality System determines the individual behavioral characteristics of an Entheon. It exists to ensure that Entheon of the same species can still develop distinct personalities, relationships and behavioral patterns. Nature and Personality are not combat statistics and do not inherently determine an Entheon’s strength. An Entheon’s: - Species - Affinity - Physical design - Species traits - Rarity - Special Move or Special Ability - Nature - Personality - Individual quirks are separate concepts and should not be treated as interchangeable.

2. Nature

Nature is the Entheon’s primary behavioral tendency. Each individual Entheon should normally have one primary Nature selected from the established Nature list. Nature describes a broad behavioral inclination rather than determining every aspect of the Entheon’s personality. For example: > Nature: Curious A Curious Entheon is naturally inclined to investigate unfamiliar objects, people and situations. However, its individual personality may determine how that curiosity manifests. A Curious Entheon could be: - Shy and hesitant - Bold and adventurous - Mischievous and intrusive - Calm and observant - Affectionate and eager to investigate alongside its trainer Nature therefore establishes a foundation rather than a rigid character archetype.

3. Nature List

Brave

Courageous and willing to face danger. A Brave Entheon is inclined to confront threats rather than retreat from them.

Timid

Cautious and easily startled. A Timid Entheon may hesitate around unfamiliar people, situations or dangers. Timidity does not prevent bravery when something important is at stake.

Calm

Composed and emotionally steady. A Calm Entheon rarely panics and generally responds to unexpected situations without excessive reaction.

Bold

Confident and assertive. A Bold Entheon is comfortable challenging others and is unlikely to back down simply because an opponent appears stronger.

Gentle

Kind and non-aggressive. A Gentle Entheon generally avoids unnecessary conflict and tends to handle other creatures carefully.

Proud

Self-confident and dignified. A Proud Entheon dislikes being underestimated, patronized or treated as inferior.

Shy

Reserved and socially cautious. A Shy Entheon may require considerable time to become comfortable around unfamiliar people or Entheon.

Curious

Fascinated by the unfamiliar. A Curious Entheon naturally investigates strange objects, unfamiliar environments and unusual situations.

Playful

Energetic and fun-loving. A Playful Entheon enjoys games, interaction and harmless forms of entertainment.

Mischievous

Enjoys tricks and harmless trouble. A Mischievous Entheon may play pranks, steal objects temporarily or deliberately provoke amusing reactions.

Affectionate

Seeks closeness and emotional connection. An Affectionate Entheon enjoys praise, attention and physical affection from trusted companions.

Loyal

Forms strong attachments to companions. A Loyal Entheon remains close to its trainer and allies and places considerable importance on established bonds.

Protective

Prioritizes the safety of others. A Protective Entheon may instinctively place itself between danger and a companion.

Independent

Prefers acting on its own judgment. An Independent Entheon may sometimes make decisions without waiting for explicit instructions, particularly when it believes it understands the situation.

Competitive

Enjoys challenges and rivalry. A Competitive Entheon is motivated by opportunities to test itself against others.

Serious

Focused and disciplined. A Serious Entheon tends to treat training, battles and important situations with considerable attention.

Energetic

Constantly active and enthusiastic. An Energetic Entheon rarely remains still for long and frequently initiates activities.

Relaxed

Easygoing and difficult to bother. A Relaxed Entheon generally takes setbacks and unexpected situations in stride.

Stubborn

Resistant to changing its mind. A Stubborn Entheon may continue pursuing an idea or course of action even after being told to stop.

Determined

Highly persistent toward goals. A Determined Entheon strongly dislikes giving up after failure and will continue attempting to overcome obstacles.

Clever

Observant and analytical. A Clever Entheon tends to look for solutions, patterns and opportunities rather than acting immediately.

Cautious

Methodical and risk-aware. A Cautious Entheon considers possible consequences before committing to an action.

Adventurous

Thrilled by exploration. An Adventurous Entheon actively seeks unfamiliar experiences and enjoys discovering new places.

Empathetic

Highly sensitive to the emotions of others. An Empathetic Entheon notices changes in the emotional state of companions and may react strongly to them.

Dignified

Naturally composed and formal. A Dignified Entheon carries itself with unusual grace and restraint.

Inquisitive

Driven to understand how things work. An Inquisitive Entheon observes, experiments and investigates mechanisms, objects and phenomena.

Free-Spirited

Strongly dislikes unnecessary restrictions. A Free-Spirited Entheon prefers freedom, improvisation and making its own choices.

Clingy

Forms exceptionally strong attachments. A Clingy Entheon strongly prefers remaining near its chosen companion and may actively seek reassurance or attention.

Stoic

Rarely displays emotion outwardly. A Stoic Entheon may care deeply about others while showing little obvious emotional reaction.

Fierce

Intense and forceful when challenged. A Fierce Entheon can become highly intimidating when confronted or when protecting something important.

Gentle-Hearted

Highly compassionate and sensitive to suffering. A Gentle-Hearted Entheon may become distressed when another creature is injured or upset.

Sly

Clever and opportunistic. A Sly Entheon naturally looks for openings and enjoys outsmarting opponents or finding unconventional solutions.

Serene

Naturally tranquil. A Serene Entheon appears unusually peaceful and is difficult to rush or provoke.

Excitable

Emotions quickly reach the surface. An Excitable Entheon reacts enthusiastically to interesting events and may display its feelings very openly.

Observant

Prefers watching before acting. An Observant Entheon frequently notices details that others overlook and may spend considerable time studying a situation before responding.

Devoted

Forms exceptionally deep bonds. A Devoted Entheon may go to considerable lengths for its chosen companions and can develop particularly strong emotional attachments.

4. Personality Expression

Nature establishes the primary behavioral tendency, but it does not completely define the individual Entheon. An Entheon should normally have one primary Nature and may receive one to three Personality Expressions. Personality Expressions describe how the Nature manifests in that individual.

Example

Nature: Timid  Personality Expressions: Affectionate, Observant Possible result: > A cautious Entheon that tends to hide behind its trainer around strangers, but closely watches everything happening around it. Once it trusts someone, it becomes surprisingly affectionate. Another individual could have: Nature: Timid  Personality Expressions: Independent, Clever Possible result: > A cautious Entheon that avoids confrontation but prefers solving problems alone rather than relying on its trainer. Both are Timid Entheon, but they are clearly different individuals.

5. Personality Expressions Are Not Additional Natures

Personality Expressions should not be treated as additional full Natures. They exist to customize how the primary Nature manifests. For example: > Nature: Proud >  Personality Expression: Affectionate does not create a contradiction. The Entheon may be dignified and dislike appearing weak while secretly enjoying praise and affection from trusted companions. Similarly: > Nature: Timid >  Personality Expression: Brave should not automatically change the Nature to Brave. Instead, it may describe an Entheon that is normally cautious but becomes courageous when protecting its trainer. Personality should therefore be capable of producing apparent contradictions. These contradictions help individual Entheon feel like actual characters rather than fixed archetypes.

6. Individual Quirks

An Entheon may additionally develop one or more Individual Quirks. Quirks are small behavioral details that make an individual memorable. Examples include: - Loves shiny objects. - Sleeps curled around its trainer’s bag. - Hates being wet. - Always tries to sit on the highest available object. - Makes small noises when concentrating. - Becomes extremely competitive about eating. - Collects interesting stones. - Copies its trainer’s gestures. - Gets embarrassed when praised. - Falls asleep during long conversations. - Constantly rearranges objects around its sleeping area. - Likes being carried despite being physically capable of walking. - Becomes fascinated by mirrors. - Always investigates new smells. - Has a favorite place to sleep. Individual Quirks should generally have no direct mechanical effect unless explicitly established elsewhere. Their purpose is characterization.

7. Species and Personality

Species characteristics may influence which Natures and Personality Expressions are plausible, but they must never determine personality automatically. An Entheon’s Affinity, appearance, species lore or rarity should not be treated as a personality shortcut. For example: - A Flame Entheon does not automatically have a fiery or aggressive personality. - An Umbral Entheon does not automatically have a sinister personality. - A Radiant Entheon does not automatically have a kind personality. - A Dragon Entheon does not automatically have a proud or dominant personality. - A Mystic Entheon does not automatically have a calm or intelligent personality. Species descriptions provide context, not restrictions. Two Entheon of the same species may have substantially different personalities.

8. Affinity and Personality

Affinity should remain mechanically and narratively separate from personality. An Entheon’s Affinity describes its elemental nature and abilities. It does not determine how the Entheon thinks or behaves. Examples: > Flame + Timid A timid Flame Entheon may be easily startled despite possessing Flame abilities. > Umbral + Affectionate An Umbral Entheon may be extremely affectionate toward its trainer. > Radiant + Mischievous A Radiant Entheon may enjoy playing harmless tricks. > Dragon + Shy A Dragon Entheon may be hesitant and reserved around unfamiliar people. > Mystic + Competitive A Mystic Entheon may actively seek out challenging opponents. Unexpected combinations are allowed and can create memorable individuals.

9. Nature and Species Development

Nature should generally remain consistent throughout an Entheon’s life, including evolution. However, Personality Expression can develop through experience. An Entheon that begins as: > Nature: Timid >  Personality: Shy, Cautious may gradually become: > Nature: Timid >  Personality: Protective, Determined after developing a strong bond with its trainer. The Entheon remains fundamentally cautious, but experience has changed how that Nature manifests. Evolution may also influence personality expression without automatically changing Nature.

10. Individual Personality Generation

  1. The Entheon’s species description.
  2. Its established species behavior.
  3. Its Affinity.
  4. Its evolutionary stage.
  5. Its rarity.
  6. Its Nature.
  7. Its Personality Expressions.
  8. Its Individual Quirks.
  9. Its experiences.
  10. Its relationship with its trainer and other Entheon. Species and Affinity should provide context, while Nature and individual experience provide the primary foundation for personality.

11. Personality Is Not Guaranteed by Species

There should be no rule stating: > “All Elvarin are dignified.” or: > “All Brinderv are gentle.” Instead, the species description establishes what is typical or aesthetically appropriate, while individual Entheon can deviate from that baseline. For example, an Elvarin may be: - Dignified and serene. - Playful and mischievous. - Shy and reserved. - Proud and competitive. - Affectionate and devoted. - Completely oblivious to how captivating others find it. All remain valid Elvarin personalities.

12. Personality and Relationships

Personality should influence how Entheon interact with: - Their trainer. - Other Entheon. - Wild Entheon. - NPCs. - Environments. - Training. - Battles. - New experiences. However, personality should not override established commands or narrative circumstances automatically. A stubborn Entheon can still follow a trusted trainer’s command. A playful Entheon can become serious during a dangerous situation. A timid Entheon can act courageously when its trainer or companion is threatened. A proud Entheon can accept help from someone it trusts. Personality creates behavioral tendencies, not absolute restrictions.

13. Nature and Battle Behavior

Nature may influence how an Entheon behaves during battle, but should not directly modify its combat statistics. For example: - A Competitive Entheon may actively seek challenging opponents. - A Cautious Entheon may favor defensive positioning. - A Fierce Entheon may readily confront threats. - A Clever Entheon may experiment with unconventional tactics. - A Protective Entheon may prioritize defending an ally. - A Timid Entheon may initially favor distance or evasive tactics. These are behavioral tendencies rather than hidden statistical modifiers.

15. Core Design Principle

Nature provides the foundation. Personality provides the character. Quirks provide individuality. Experience provides development.

An Entheon should therefore never feel like: > “It is a Flame Entheon, so it is aggressive.” Instead, it should feel like: > “It is a Flame Entheon with a Timid Nature, an affectionate personality and an embarrassing habit of hiding behind its trainer whenever someone compliments it.” The species determines what it is. The Nature influences how it tends to behave. The Personality determines who this individual is. The Quirks make it memorable. The Entheon’s experiences determine who it may eventually become.

Species-Specific Move Pools

This document defines the ordinary moves available to each of the 100 established creatures and the level at which each move is learned naturally.

How to read the move tables

  • Species Move Pool: The moves this species can potentially learn through its ordinary progression.
  • Level: The level at which the move becomes available through natural level progression.
  • Individual Moveset: A separate concept. An individual Entheon does not necessarily know every move in its species pool simultaneously.
  • Special Moves/Abilities: Separate from ordinary moves and do not occupy an ordinary move slot.
  • Nature/Personality: Separate from both ordinary moves and Special Moves/Abilities.

Level Progression Rules

  • Levels range from 1–100.
  • Each species begins with its earliest listed moves at Level 1.
  • Later moves unlock progressively as the individual levels.
  • Evolutionary stages receive progressively broader and more advanced learnsets.
  • The listed levels define the current canonical progression for these species.
  • The progression system tracks each individual’s current level and learned moves.
  • A newly available move does not automatically require an existing move to be forgotten; the active move-limit rules will determine how the individual manages its moveset.
  • This document defines ordinary level-up acquisition only. Other acquisition methods can be added later without changing the species’ core pool.
  • Special Moves/Abilities are intentionally excluded from these tables.

Leveling & Evolution System

1. Purpose

The Leveling and Evolution System governs how individual Entheon gain experience, increase in level, learn ordinary moves, and become eligible to evolve. Level and evolution are properties of the individual Entheon, while evolution requirements are defined by the species. The progression system tracks each individual Entheon’s progression.

2. Level System

Every individual Entheon has a Level from 1 to 100. Level represents the Entheon’s overall development, training and accumulated experience. Level affects: - Access to level-up moves. - Evolution eligibility. - General combat development. - Other species-specific progression where explicitly defined. Level does not automatically determine personality, Nature or Special Move/Ability.

3. Experience Points

The XP required to reach the next level follows the formula: > XP Required = 100 + (Current Level × 20) Examples:

Current Level XP Required for Next Level
1 120
5 200
10 300
15 400
20 500
25 600
30 700
40 900
50 1,100
60 1,300
70 1,500
80 1,700
90 1,900
99 2,080

When an Entheon gains enough XP to level up, any excess XP carries over toward the following level.

4. Sources of Experience

XP represents meaningful development rather than simply the number of opponents defeated.

4.1 Battle Experience

An Entheon can gain XP by: - Participating meaningfully in a battle. - Defeating an opponent. - Helping defeat an opponent. - Successfully overcoming a significantly stronger opponent. The amount of XP should consider: - The Entheon’s level. - The opponent’s level. - The relative difficulty of the encounter. - The Entheon’s actual participation. An Entheon should not receive the same reward for defeating a vastly weaker opponent as it would for overcoming a meaningful challenge.

4.2 Training Experience

Entheon can gain XP through meaningful training. Examples include: - Practicing moves. - Physical conditioning. - Learning to control an Affinity. - Training against a partner. - Successfully mastering a difficult technique. Training generally provides less XP than a genuinely challenging battle, but allows Entheon to develop without requiring constant combat.

4.3 Significant Experience

Meaningful story events can also contribute XP. Examples include: - Protecting a trainer or ally during a dangerous event. - Overcoming a significant personal obstacle. - Successfully applying something learned through previous training. - Surviving or overcoming a particularly difficult situation. XP should reflect meaningful development, not arbitrary narrative rewards.

5. Anti-Grinding Rule

Repeatedly defeating extremely weak opponents should provide progressively reduced XP. This prevents trivial encounters from becoming an efficient method of unlimited progression. Experience gained reflects the relative level and difficulty of the encounter. The system should reward meaningful challenges and development, not repetitive farming.

6. Level-Up Procedure

  1. Increase the Entheon’s Level by 1.
  2. Carry excess XP into the next level.
  3. Check the species’ Level-Up Learnset.
  4. Check whether an evolution requirement has been satisfied.
  5. Check for any other species-specific progression.
  6. Update the Entheon’s hidden progression state. If the new level corresponds to a move in the species’ Level-Up Learnset, that move becomes available for the Entheon to learn.

7. Evolution System

Evolution is a species-specific transformation that changes an Entheon into a predetermined evolutionary form. Each evolutionary species has a fixed evolution path. Evolution always follows the established species data. For example: > Nimblet → Nymbril → Nymbrake Nimblet cannot randomly evolve into another species.

8. Evolution Eligibility

Meeting an evolution requirement makes evolution available. It does not automatically force the evolution to occur. > [Entheon] is ready to evolve into Evolution. Would you like to proceed with its evolution? This should function similarly to the classic Entheon game mechanic where the player can choose whether to allow an evolution to proceed. The player may: - Proceed with evolution. - Decline/delay evolution. If evolution is delayed, the Entheon remains in its current form and can continue gaining levels normally. Once an evolution requirement has been satisfied, the requirement remains satisfied until the evolution is resolved. The player can choose to evolve the Entheon later.

9. Standard Level-Based Evolution

The following evolutionary lines use level-based evolution.

Entheon Evolves Into Requirement
Nimblet Nymbril Level 16
